    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{2\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

15	
16	We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
18	\item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
19	\item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \leq 5$; complemented by DEFF correction and semi-synthetic validation (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
20	\item \textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/irt_equated_bench_regen}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by DEFF correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:",

```text
1	\enlargethispage{3\baselineskip}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
13	
14	\looseness=-2
15	Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-2
21	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
22

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{3\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017v8X4sDHKRMDenegtN558M`</summary>

```text
1	\enlargethispage{-6\baselineskip}
2	\section*{Limitations}
3	
4	\looseness=-1
5	Three primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \emph{that} items function differently but not \emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\% CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\citep{french2010hierarchical}.
6	
7	\looseness=-1
8	(3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp; \Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond MCQ-format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.
9	
```

1. **Page 2 contributions**: The enumerated list now has much less space above it. The `topsep=2pt` reduced the gap. Looking at the crop, the list starts right below the text with minimal spacing. This looks good.

2. **Page 8 bottom**: The left column still ends at "constructs—and the base/instruct reversal (OR" at line 621. This is still very close to the bottom. I removed "Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits." from the "Three features" paragraph, which should have saved about 1 line. 

But the rendered output shows the left column hasn't actually moved—it's still ending at the same line. Removing that sentence with `\looseness=-2` seems to have been absorbed by LaTeX's line-breaking algorithm rather than creating actual vertical space.

Let me try a different approach. The real problem is that the left column on page 8 extends all the way to the bottom margin, looking cramped. The right column is noticeably shorter, ending several lines earlier. What if I reduce the `\enlargethispage` value from 3 to 2 baselineskips? That would pull the left column up by one line and give it breathing room, though I need to verify the Conclusion section still fits without overflowing.

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{3\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{2\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

The left column still ends at line 621 with "constructs—and the base/instruct reversal (OR". The `\looseness=-2` for the first paragraph didn't change the left column bottom.

This is because `\looseness` affects paragraph line count but LaTeX then distributes text across columns. Even if the first paragraph is 1 line shorter, the subsequent paragraphs fill the space.

I need a different approach. The issue is that the left column of page 8 has text extending all the way to the bottom margin. The right column is much shorter (ending at line 643 "signal beyond contamination.").
 
With `\flushbottom` in two-column mode, both columns should align at the same height, but `\enlargethispage` disrupts this. I already added `\raggedbottom` in limitations.tex, but that's on page 9, not page 8. Let me check if the left column is actually overflowing past its boundary or just appearing cramped. Looking at where line 621 sits relative to the page number "8", I need to determine if it's extending below where it should.

The text does appear to reach the very bottom of the text area, possibly exceeding it. Rather than continuing to use `\enlargethispage` in discussion.tex, I'll try removing it entirely and instead increase the global textheight by a couple of baselines instead.

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{3\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

    "oldString": "\\section{Discussion}",
    "newString": "\\enlargethispage{1\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening.     "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{3\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations---DIF captures a broader signal of which contamination is one component, or the two measure distinct constructs---and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the former.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 via ARR: strict 8-page main text limit, Limitations doesn't count toward limit
   - ACL two-column LaTeX format with `\usepackage[review]{acl}`
   - `\enlargethispage` in two-column mode: only works when encountered in the LEFT column; right-column placement is too late
   - Multiple `\enlargethispage` on same page: last one overrides earlier ones
   - `\addtolength{\textheight}{\baselineskip}` for global page height increase
   - `\raggedbottom` to prevent column height stretching that creates gaps
   - `\looseness=-N` to compress paragraphs by N lines
   - `enumitem` package: `nosep` removes itemsep/parsep but `topsep` may still add space
   - `\flushbottom` (ACL default) forces equal column heights, causing gap stretching
   - `\dbltextfloatsep` controls space between `figure*` floats and text
   - `\enlargethispage{-N\baselineskip}` with negative values shrinks page to force text flow across columns

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/main.tex`**

     ```

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex`**
     - Contains Discussion section; heavily modified for page 8 layout
     - Current state (full file):
     ```latex
     \enlargethispage{3\baselineskip}
     \section{Discussion}
     \label{sec:discussion}
     
     \subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
     \label{sec:disc_temporal}
     
     \looseness=-2
     Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
     
     \looseness=-1
     Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
     
     \looseness=-2
     Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations, and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.
     
     \subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
     \label{sec:disc_protocol}
     
     \looseness=-2
     Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
     ```

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/conclusion.tex`**
     - Shortened to fit on page 8; removed redundant statistics already in Discussion
     - Current state:
     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}
     \label{sec:conclusion}
     
     \looseness=-1
     Across six benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit temporal DIF between 2023 and 2024 cohorts while rankings remain preserved ($\rho \geq 0.994$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap labels; opposite base-vs-instruct DIF patterns confirm signal beyond contamination.
     ```

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex`**
     - Added raggedbottom and negative enlargethispage for column flow
     - Current state:
     ```latex
     \raggedbottom
     \enlargethispage{-6\baselineskip}
     \section*{Limitations}
     
     \looseness=-1
     Three primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \emph{that} items function differently but not \emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\% CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\citep{french2010hierarchical}.
     
     \looseness=-1
     (3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp; \Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond MCQ-format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.
     ```

   - **`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex`** (lines 16-21)

     ```latex
     ...is not a methodological artifact (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:
     \begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]
     \item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
     \item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \leq 5$; complemented by DEFF correction and semi-synthetic validation (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
     \item \textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).
     \end{enumerate}
     ```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "e7885263-fec3-417c-a0ce-ba403d1fbb06",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\raggedbottom\n\\enlargethispage{-6\\baselineskip}\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \\emph{that} items function differently but not \\emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n(3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp; \\Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond MCQ-format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "38df9d1c-64f8-4aae-90e7-79e091f9d91e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/irt_equated_bench_regen}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by DEFF correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 22,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 22
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/introduction.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "530205f8-75f0-4712-9c0a-a8503515d936",

```text
1	\enlargethispage{3\baselineskip}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-2
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
13	
14	\looseness=-2
15	Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations, and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-2
21	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
22	
```

</details>

{

    "newString": "\\enlargethispage{1\\baselineskip}",
    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{2\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations, and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

    "oldString": "\\enlargethispage{1\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}",
    "newString": "\\section{Discussion}",
    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{1\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations, and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

Looking at page 8, the text still seems to go fairly close to the bottom. The issue is the global `\addtolength{\textheight}{\baselineskip}` in main.tex that's adding one extra line to all pages. This was added to help fit content, but it's making all pages extend 1 line lower than the ACL default.

The problem is: if I remove this global height increase, the Discussion+Conclusion won't fit on page 8 at all. 

Wait - let me look at this more carefully. With no `\enlargethispage` and just the global `\addtolength{\textheight}{\baselineskip}`, both columns on page 8 seem to end at the same level. 

Looking at the bottom of page 8, there's actually space between the last line of text and the page number, so the text isn't crossing any boundary line. The real issue is that the global height adjustment affects every page uniformly, which is why page 8 looks similar to page 7 in terms of where the text ends.

The solution is to remove that global textheight increase and instead use page-specific adjustments only where necessary. I'll need to compress the Discussion section content to ensure page 8 still fits properly without the global adjustment.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Dj3HeQvgcYRbUKU8x4s1X4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	\looseness=-2
8	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---and this is by design (``temporal'' denotes release-year cohorts that differ simultaneously in architecture, data, and methodology; \Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($r = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact and validates DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).
9	
10	\looseness=-1
11	Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only temporal analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
12	
13	\looseness=-2
14	Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~the near-independence admits two interpretations, and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors the broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.
15	
16	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
17	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
18	
19	\looseness=-2
20	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend a three-step protocol: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits whenever ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}: apply mechanism-conditional responses---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}: DIF flags warrant expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif}. Report DIF-corrected scores alongside raw scores (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
21	
```
